Read `docs/PRD.md` and `docs/architecture.md`. Here's what I heard.

**Your flow:**
1. Photo, then an "analyzing" screen.
2. Nutrition numbers appear: calories etc.
3. Duo streak with a friend, shown on the same result.
4. User answers Food and Feel questions after the meal.
5. Over time, the app learns each user's likes, dislikes and what suits their body.
6. The app then gives recipes based on that.

**Where this differs from the PRD:**
- **Recipes are out of scope** in the PRD ("meal plans and recipes"). You describe them as the payoff.
- **"Trains the AI"** doesn't match the PRD. Feel is a simple rule (a pattern needs at least 60% of check-ins in one answer and at least 3 check-ins). Gemini only rewrites the flagged pattern into one sentence. No model is trained.
- **Nutrition source:** the PRD says FatSecret, with the local desi table as fallback. You're replacing that with the Pakistani research paper data, FAO/INFoods and USDA.
- **Feel questions:** the PRD asks them 90 minutes after a saved meal, not at scan time. You may have meant that.

**My guess, not yours:** "AI knows the user" would be a profile of liked and disliked dishes plus Feel patterns, fed to Gemini as context. No model training.

Are recipes now in scope for the hackathon, and if so, are they a must-ship or a stretch?

Status: research done on the main sources. Here's what I found, and what I couldn't verify.

**Verified**
- **Paper:** "Developing a Machine Learning–Based System for Nutrient Profiling of Pakistani Cuisine," *Nutrients* 2026.
  - It covers 83 dishes in 10 categories, with calories, protein, carbs, fat and sodium.
  - Values are per 100 g of cooked dish.
  - They are calculated from recipes using USDA data, not lab-measured. The authors say their cross-check against other tools was qualitative only.
  - There is no per-serving or katori size, so we'd need a grams-per-portion table ourselves.
- **USDA FoodData Central:** CC0, 1,000 requests/hour per key. Good for ingredient-level backup.
- **FAO Pakistan table:** from 1985, a 70-page document. The page gives no download format or foods count, so it's likely not machine-readable. I'd skip it.
- **pakistani.recipes JSON:** it has titles, region, times, servings and an ingredient *count*. It has no ingredients, steps, nutrition or license. It can't power recipes as is.
- **Gemini:**
  - It takes JPEG, PNG, WEBP, HEIC and HEIF, and structured JSON output is supported.
  - A free tier exists on several Flash and Flash-Lite models.
  - Free-tier content is marked "used to improve our products: Yes", so users' food photos would feed Google.
  - Paid Flash-Lite is $0.25 per 1M input tokens, and each image costs about 258 tokens per tile.
- **Grok (xAI):**
  - Image input works on some models, but only JPG and PNG, so HEIC would need converting.
  - Enum-constrained JSON output is supported.
  - The cheapest model I saw listed that fits our use is $2 in / $6 out per 1M tokens (grok-4.7).
  - I couldn't confirm which models accept images or what free credits exist today. Third-party pages conflict.

**Couldn't verify**
- The Kaggle image count, class list and license. The page didn't load for me.
- The supplementary nutrition file. The download returned 403 to curl.
- Gemini's free-tier request limits. They're only shown inside AI Studio.

**My read, not fact:** training our own model isn't decided yet. It hinges on the Kaggle image count, which I don't have. Gemini fits the PRD best, since it picks from our dish list in structured output. Grok is costlier and has less documentation for what we need. I'd settle this with a small test: run 50 to 100 labelled photos through the candidate models.

Want me to open the Kaggle page and the supplementary download in your Chrome browser to get the image count, dish list and nutrition file?

**Kaggle dataset (read in Chrome, no login needed)**
- Name: SmartFoodAnalyzer-Dataset. 14.7k files, 2.15 GB, 63 class folders at about 240 images each.
- **License: "Unknown"**, and the description is blank. That's a risk for anything we ship.
- **The paper says 83 dishes. The dataset has 63 folders**, so they don't line up.
- I saw 30 of the 63 class names. They include generic items like `chai_tea`, `coffee`, `fries`, `fish`.
- `falooda` has 1 image, and `aloo_gosht` has 139.
- My read, not fact: about 240 images per class is enough to fine-tune a small classifier. Even so, the unknown license, the PRD marking model training out of scope, and the 63-vs-83 mismatch all weigh against it.

**Groq**
- Its only documented vision model is `qwen/qwen3.8-27b`, and it's **Preview**. Groq's docs say preview models are evaluation only, not for production.
- Max 3 images per request, and JSON mode is documented for images. JSON-schema enums, which we need to lock dishes to our catalog, are not documented for images.
- Price: $0.80 in / $4.00 out per 1M tokens. Free-tier vision limits weren't visible.
- Data: official docs say any customer can turn on zero data retention. Third-party pages conflict, so read the legal terms yourself before relying on that.
- Net: Groq is weaker than Gemini for this unless a test says otherwise.

**Still unverified**
- **Supplementary nutrition file:** the link is `https://www.mdpi.com/article/10.3390/nu18193173/s1`, a 655 KB zip from MDPI. Plain curl got a 403, so a browser download is the way.
- **Gemini free-tier limits:** only visible in your AI Studio.

**Keys**

**Gemini free limits (your sheet)**

| Model | Req/min | Req/day |
|---|---|---|
| 3.1 Flash-Lite, 3.5 Flash-Lite | 15 | 500 |

| 2.5 Flash, 3 Flash, 3.6 Flash, 3.8 Flash | 5 | 20 |
| 2.5 Pro, 3.1 Pro | 0 | 0 |

- All Flash-Lite and Flash rows get 250K tokens/min. Gemma 4 gets 16K.
- Only the two Flash-Lite models (3.1 and 3.5) have a usable daily budget. The regular Flash models, at 20 a day, can't run an eval or serve testers.
- My arithmetic, not a measured fact: PRD usage of 2 to 3 scans per user per day on 500 requests/day covers roughly 150 to 250 tester-days. That quota is shared with our own dev and eval runs.

**Groq:** your key only lists `qwen/qwen3.8-27b` as a possible vision model, and it's Preview. That makes Groq a distant second.

Supplementary file read. The zip holds one 14-page PDF, and it changes the picture.

**What the PDF shows**
- **Nutrition table:** about 59 dishes by my count, not the 83 in the paper. It gives per-100g calories, protein, carbs, fat and sodium. The values are calculated from USDA, and a cross-check on 15 dishes had mean deviation of 8.4%.
- **No portion sizes:** nothing says how many grams a roti, katori or plate is. The table has no fiber column, and raita appears only in an example, not in the table.
- **Images are scraped:** they come from web scraping, YouTube thumbnails, volunteer phone photos and about 2,000 Indian-dataset images. That matches the Kaggle license "Unknown".
- **Their own model struggled:** the authors say single-stage YOLOv8-seg has "low" classification accuracy on similar foods, and their example mislabels fries as `meethee_chawal`. They report no portion-error number.
- **Their "continuous learning":** it means logging user corrections and retraining the model periodically. No per-user model.

**My recommended design (my guess; tell me what's wrong)**
1. **Dish recognition:** Gemini 3.1 Flash-Lite picks dishes from our catalog as an enum plus "unknown". No training: the authors' own results suggest we'd struggle in 10 days, and the PRD puts it out of scope.
2. **Nutrition:** the paper's table is our local source, with per-100g values per catalog dish. USDA is only for gaps. FatSecret is dropped.
3. **Portions:** we write a small grams-per-serving table ourselves (roti, katori, plate), since no source has it. Plan-engine math is the paper's formula: nutrient = per-100g × grams / 100.
4. **"Learning the user":** save every correction and Feel answer. Per-user likes and dislikes go to Gemini as context for recipes (stretch goal).
5. **Evaluation:** test candidate models on 50 to 100 labelled photos before locking anything in.

The PDF and zip are sitting in the repo root. I'd move them out of git-tracked space, not commit them.

The PRD wants a 30 to 40 dish catalog. Should I propose it from the dishes that appear in both the paper's table and the Kaggle class list, for you to trim?

Research is done. **.gitignore:** both downloads are now ignored. I also added `Gemini-API - Sheet1.csv`, which you didn't ask for. Say so if you want it back out.

**Better data exists, but only for part of the menu**

1. **Lab-measured Pakistani dishes (best find).** Khan et al. 2019, *Progress in Nutrition*, has 30 home-style dishes from Khyber Pakhtunkhwa.
   - Five housewives' recipes per dish were averaged, then analysed with AOAC lab methods, in duplicate.
   - It gives per-100g fat, protein, carbs and energy.
   - The dishes include chicken biryani, chicken and mutton karahi, nihari, haleem, chapli kabab, aloo gosht, korma, pulao, bhindi, palak and daals.
   - The text doesn't state a license, and it's one province and one lab.
2. **Indian Nutrient Databank (INDB).** It has 1,014 recipes with per-100g and per-serving values.
   - The paper is CC BY, but the GitHub repo has no license file.
   - It's calculated, not measured, and it isn't Pakistani.
   - I haven't checked which dishes it has.
3. **Nothing better turned up.** I found no current digital Pakistani composition table. The official one dates from 1985, with a 2001 revision. The web calorie charts disagree with each other, so I wouldn't trust them.

**The paper's table runs lower than the lab on several home-style dishes (kcal per 100g):**

| Dish | Paper table (USDA-calculated) | Lab-measured |
|---|---|---|
| Bhindi | 85 | 158 |
| Mixed veg | 90 | 142 |
| Maash daal | 172 | 272 |
| Chicken biryani | 195 | 160 |
| Haleem | 165 | 128 |
| Chicken karahi | 200 | 241 |
| Nihari | 220 | 172 |

Chapli kebab matches (280 vs 279). My read: the vegetable dishes differ because home cooking uses more oil than the calculated recipes assume. The other differences go both ways.

**Proposed data plan**
- **Lab value first.** Use the lab figure when a dish is in the 30-dish study.
- **Paper table next.** Use the paper's per-100g table for the remaining dishes, marked "calculated".
- **INDB for gaps.** Use it only for dishes in neither, if its names fit.
- **Catalog.** Every dish with data from at least one source, roughly 70 by my count. I'll check later whether that many look-alike dishes hurts Gemini's accuracy.
- **Portions.** No source has katori or roti sizes, so we write a small grams-per-serving table ourselves.
- **Database.** One table: dish, per-100g values, source, lab or calculated. The UI says "estimate" everywhere.

INDB downloaded and checked. Data is decided below.

**What INDB gives us**
- 1,014 recipes. **No** chicken biryani, karahi, nihari, haleem, aloo gosht, chapli kebab, paye, jalebi, falooda or zarda. It covers the Pakistan-specific dishes poorly.
- It does have roti, naan, paratha, pulao, mutton biryani, korma, kheer, halwa, raita, lassi, kulfi, khichdi, daals, saag, bhindi, fish curry and others.
- **Serving units are the useful part.** Each recipe lists a unit (chapati, naan, plate, bowl, samosa) and kcal per unit. Dividing that by kcal per 100g gives grams per unit. My arithmetic, not stated in the data: a chapati is about 36 g, a naan about 53 g, a fish-curry bowl about 254 g.
- **Fried items are broken.** A potato samosa shows 814 kcal per samosa, gulab jamun 918 per serving, and chicken kebab 729 kcal/100g. It looks like all the frying oil is counted as eaten. The paper's authors list "yield not corrected" as a limitation. I'd exclude these rows.
- Roti is 202 kcal/100g in INDB, against 265 in the paper's table, so the sources differ there too.

**Final data plan**
1. **Lab values** (Khan 2019, 30 dishes) win wherever a dish is covered.
2. **The paper's table** (about 59 dishes) fills the rest, tagged `calculated`.
3. **INDB, hand-picked only:** roti, naan, paratha, pulao, kheer, halwa, raita, lassi, kulfi, khichdi, daals, saag and fish curry. Never its fried or kofta rows.
4. **Portions:** grams per unit derived from INDB where it covers the dish. For karahi, nihari and haleem I'd start with a default bowl size (my assumption, to be calibrated).
5. **One table** in Supabase: dish, per-100g values, grams per unit, source and basis. The seed file is built once by script.
6. **Catalog:** every dish that ends up with a value. The Kaggle images are used only for our eval, and we copy facts and attribute them.
7. Reuse questions: the lab paper's license is unstated and the INDB repo has none. The INDB paper says CC BY. I'm not a lawyer, so we should credit all three sources.

Does this data plan look right, or is there anything you'd change before I move on to the AI and backend design?

I read the repo. Part of the backend is already scaffolded, so here's how the design lands on it. My guesses are marked.

**Scan flow**
1. The app uploads a photo. `scan` resizes it and sends it to **Gemini 3.1 Flash-Lite**, with the catalog as an enum plus `unknown`. It returns dish slug, portion (a unit count, like 2 rotis) and confidence. No calories.
2. Low-confidence or `unknown` results come back as correction chips, and the user confirms.
3. `nutrition` reads our own table, with no external call. The `plan` module fits portions to the remaining budget, and `meals` saves predicted and confirmed values. `meal_items` already stores both.

**What changes in the repo**
- `dishes` currently has FatSecret fields and per-unit calories. It becomes per-100g values plus grams per unit, `source` and `basis`.
- `nutrition/fatsecret.client.ts` goes away, and `local-table.ts` reads from the database.
- ADR 0002 gets superseded, and the seed is generated from our three sources.
- `gemini.client.ts` is still a stub. It's the main AI work.

**Guesses**
- A per-user daily scan cap protects the shared 500 requests/day. Over the cap, the user enters dishes by hand.
- Feel stays rule-based as in the PRD. Gemini only rewrites a flagged pattern into one sentence.
- "Learning the user" needs no model training. It's a profile built from saved meals, corrections and Feel answers. The profile is passed to Gemini as context for stretch-goal recipes, from a small open recipe set.
- Training our own model stays out for the MVP.

The PRD doesn't say how the app learns what a user likes or dislikes. Should that come only from what they log and how they feel, or can they also tap like/dislike on dishes and recipes?

I found plenty on how apps *ask* for preferences and less on how they *learn* them. The sources are thin and several are blogs or marketing, so I've flagged what's weak.

**What existing apps do**
- **Ask up front (well supported):** Yummly, Mealime, Eat This Much and PlateJoy ask for diet, disliked ingredients and favourite cuisines at signup. One Reddit user says Eat This Much still showed foods they'd excluded, which is anecdotal.
- **Learn from taps:** Yummly says the recipes you "Yum" feed your taste profile. Its current weighting isn't documented.
- **Learn from history (unconfirmed):** MyFitnessPal and Lose It reportedly rank recent and frequent foods. I found no official docs.
- **Feel tracking:** Bearable starts showing a food-and-mood pattern after at least 3 days with a factor and 3 without. Its team suggests about 100 days of data for a strong result, and says correlation isn't causation. Your PRD rule (3+ check-ins, 60%) is in the same spirit.
- **Research:**
  - Food recommenders have mostly used explicit feedback.
  - Inferring dislike from silence is unreliable, because not eating a dish doesn't mean disliking it.
  - A 796-person food-delivery survey found few people leave explicit feedback.
  - LLM preference benchmarks (PersonaMem, PrefEval) test how well models remember user preferences over long chats. I haven't verified how they score.

**Proposed approach (my inference from the above)**
1. **Cold start:** one onboarding step for avoided dishes and ingredients, such as no beef. These are hard filters enforced in code, not left to the prompt.
2. **Implicit:** dishes eaten often count as a weak "likes". Silence never counts as dislike.
3. **Feel:** the PRD rule flags per-dish or per-category patterns. Copy says "you often felt light after this", not "suits your body", to respect the no-medical-advice and "pattern, never cause" guardrails.
4. **One-tap "not for me"** on recipe cards, since that's the only place real dislikes show up.
5. **Recipes:** filter and rank from the open recipe set in code, from a compact per-user profile. Gemini only words the explanation.

Are you OK with a short avoid-list step at onboarding plus a one-tap "not for me" on recipe cards?

Sources: [Yummly onboarding](https://blog.prototypr.io/user-onboarding-spotlight-why-yummlys-ux-is-absolutely-delicious-46956e9bd8ef), [Mealime personalization](https://work.mealime.com/personalization), [Bearable correlations](https://bearable.notion.site/Discover-what-s-improving-or-worsening-your-health-89c7b083fcc2491f917e6f70d545b28b), [food-delivery feedback survey](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s11257-023-09385-8), [food recommender review](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/320944468_Food_Recommender_Systems_Important_Contributions_Challenges_and_Future_Research_Directions), [PrefEval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.09597), [PersonaMem](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2504.14225).

**What I actually based "no training" on**
- The Kaggle dataset has an unknown license and blank provenance.
- The paper's authors say their single-stage model had low accuracy on similar foods, and they report no portion-error figure.
- The PRD calls training out of scope, and we have 10 days.
- Nobody has measured Gemini on these dishes either.

**What I didn't check**
- Published accuracy of fine-tuned classifiers on Pakistani or South Asian food.
- Whether Gemini supports fine-tuning on our key.
- Open vision models you listed (Qwen3-VL via Ollama, YOLO, MiDaS, Nutrition5k).
- Any real test of Gemini zero-shot on these dishes.

**What I believe but haven't verified (general knowledge):**
- **A trained classifier** has no quota and runs fast on our own server. But its classes are fixed, plates with several dishes need detection or segmentation, and it can fall apart on real phone photos unlike its training images.
- **Gemini zero-shot** handles several dishes and "unknown" naturally, and needs no training or hosting. But accuracy on desi dishes is unproven, and the quota is tight.
- **A fair test:** the Kaggle images are scraped and probably include near-duplicates. A model trained and tested on them could look better than it would on real meal photos. A fair test needs 20 to 30 team-taken photos.

**A real comparison I can run (both parts need your OK, since downloads and training time are involved):**
1. Research published results and Gemini's tuning options.
2. Run a head-to-head: fine-tune a small pretrained classifier on a Kaggle subset, and score it and Gemini on the same held-out and team-photo sets.

Do you want me to run part 1 only, or both?

Part 1 research is done and the scripts are ready. I'm blocked only on the dataset file, which the browser tool can't save.

**Part 1: research (published evidence, mostly indirect)**
- **No direct Pakistani result.** I found no published accuracy for a fine-tuned classifier on Pakistani food. Similar fine-tuned CNNs on small local cuisines reach roughly mid-80s to mid-90s top-1 (Indonesian 94.5%, Central Asian about 88%). Those are other cuisines and class counts.
- **Zero-shot models on Indian food:** one study of open vision-language models found 31 to 51% accuracy, and one mistook chapati for tortilla. GPT-4V reached 87.5% on general food *detection*. I found no published Gemini number for desi dishes.
- **Gemini fine-tuning exists, but not on your free key.** Google's docs list supervised tuning with images for Gemini 3.1 Flash-Lite and 2.5 Flash-Lite, through the paid Vertex AI platform. It needs a Google Cloud project and billing, and I haven't priced it.
- **Read:** published evidence leans toward fine-tuned classifiers winning on fixed dishes, so my "no training" call is weaker than I stated. The head-to-head will settle it.

**Part 2: the experiment (this Mac has an M1 Pro, 16 GB, GPU enabled in PyTorch)**
- It uses a 63-class EfficientNet-B0 fine-tune against Gemini zero-shot on the same held-out photos.
- Near-duplicate images are grouped so no test image has a twin in training.
- The scripts and the Python environment live in my scratchpad folder, outside the repo.

**What I need from you:** the Kaggle tab is open in your Chrome, so please click Download then "Download dataset as zip" (2 GB) and save it to `~/Downloads`. Tell me when it's done and I'll unzip it into my scratchpad and run everything.

Also useful: 20 to 30 real phone photos of meals, even a few, for a fairer test than scraped images.

Training is on epoch 4 of 6, about 3.5 minutes per epoch, so roughly 8 more minutes.

Test accuracy on the same 305 held-out images, by epoch:

| Epoch | Top-1 | Top-5 |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | 57.0% | 85.6% |
| 2 | 66.2% | 90.8% |
| 3 | 75.1% | 95.4% |

- **Gemini zero-shot:** 65.4% top-1.
- **Fine-tuned EfficientNet-B0:** passed Gemini by epoch 3 and is still improving. The learning rate is still high, so epochs 4 to 6 usually add several more points.
- **Caveat:** these are scraped Kaggle images, which favor a classifier trained on the same kind of photos. The team-photo test is what settles this.

I'll send the final numbers when training finishes.

[CLAUDE_LOG_ENTRY type=RESPONSE num=24 session=1552c292-3466-48ec-bbc1-536015265170]
timestamp: 2026-11-01T14:34:57.087Z
time: 2026-11-01 14:34
chars: 2416

Both runs are done. The fine-tuned small classifier beat Gemini zero-shot by about 18 points on the same photos.

| | Top-1 | Top-5 | Speed | Size |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| EfficientNet-B0, fine-tuned (6 epochs, ~25 min on your Mac) | **83.6%** | 94.1% | 8 ms/image | 16 MB |
| Gemini 3.1 Flash-Lite, zero-shot | **65.4%** | not measured | 2.7 s/image | API |

- **Same-image comparison:** on the 298 images where both answered, the classifier scored 83.2% and Gemini 65.4%. The classifier was right and Gemini wrong on 72 images. The reverse happened on 19, and both were wrong on 31.
- **Gemini's confidence is not reliable.** Even at confidence 0.9 or above it was right only 70% of the time, and 17 answers were "unknown".
- **Both models confuse look-alikes.** Gemini mixed up paye vs. nihari and tikka vs. malai boti. The classifier confused chai vs. coffee, saag vs. palak gosht, and sheermal vs. puri.

**What this does not prove:**
- **Scraped test photos favor the classifier.** The train and test images come from the same scraped source. Real phone photos of home meals will score lower, for both models. This is why I still need your 20 to 30 team photos.
- **The classifier handles one dish per photo.** It can't say "none of these" or name several dishes on a plate. Detecting multiple dishes needs a detector, which costs more labelling work. Gemini does multi-dish natively.
- **The test is small.** 305 images means about ±4 points of noise. The 18-point gap is well outside that.
- **The Gemini run was zero-shot.** It saw only class names. Adding a short description per dish might lift it a few points, but I doubt it closes 18.

**My read:** training is both doable and better on this test. Per your rule ("if training is better and doable, go for it"), the plan would be:
- Train and host the classifier on the backend, with no per-scan API cost or quota.
- Use Gemini only as a fallback for low-confidence or multi-dish photos. That is my suggestion, not tested.

The 16 MB model file is small enough to run inside the Node backend or a small Python service, but I haven't checked which fits your Supabase and Express setup. This is still your decision.

Next, if you agree:
1. You send the team photos. I score both models on them, and that result decides it.
2. I update the design doc sections 4 and 8 with these numbers.

Do you want to send photos first, or decide on the classifier now?

The result flipped: on web photos Gemini beat the classifier by 30 points. This undercuts my last message, so I'm withdrawing the recommendation I gave there.

**Web photos (38 photos from Wikipedia, 33 answered by both models)**

| | Correct | Top-1 |
|---|---|---|
| Gemini 3.1 Flash-Lite, zero-shot | 31 of 33 | 94% |
| Fine-tuned EfficientNet-B0 | 21 of 33 | 64% (65.8% over all 38) |

- Gemini was right and the classifier wrong on 12 photos. The reverse happened on 2. Both were wrong on none.
- Gemini's 5 failed calls were network errors. I excluded them from this comparison.
- The classifier's misses here are bad: haleem called rabri, nihari called pickle, fish called samosa. That points to it overfitting to how the Kaggle photos look.

**Both tests together**

| Test | Classifier | Gemini |
|---|---|---|
| Kaggle held-out, 305 images | 83.6% | 65.4% |
| Web photos, 33 images | 64% | 94% |

**What I can and can't conclude**
- **The classifier is good only on photos that look like its training data.** Real users will send phone photos that look nothing like scraped web images, so the web result is closer to production.
- **The Kaggle test mostly measures how well a model learned that dataset's quirks.** Gemini's 65% there likely includes noisy or ambiguous labels in scraped data. I haven't audited this.
- **The web result is flattering to Gemini.** Wikipedia lead photos are famous and clean, and Gemini may have seen them in training. Only 33 photos is also a small test.
- **Neither test is real home meals.** Messy plates, bad lighting, and several dishes per photo are untested.

**Revised recommendation**
- **Gemini 3.1 Flash-Lite is the default scanner.**
  - It generalizes better on this evidence.
  - It handles several dishes in one photo.
  - It needs no model hosting.
  - Its free-tier limits fit the cap of 10 scans per user per day.
- **Fine-tuning stays an option, not the plan.** If real photos show Gemini struggling on specific dishes, then I'd add training, probably a classifier trained on more varied photos than Kaggle alone.
- **This reverses what you just agreed to.** You approved "train it" based on the Kaggle numbers alone. Please re-confirm with the web numbers in front of you.

The decision is firmer if I test on real home photos. You said you can't provide the team's, and I can't get truly home-style photos from the web. A cheap alternative is for you or a teammate to snap about 15 photos of ordinary meals over the next few days and drop them into a folder. Then I run the same test in a few minutes.

Which do you want?
1. Go with Gemini now, and I update the design doc sections 4 and 8 with all these numbers.
2. Wait for the home photos first.

Finalised means you chose it, as the doc marks with "Decided". Everything else is my call.

**You decided**
- Nutrition data comes from Pakistani research instead of FatSecret. The precedence is lab measurements, then the paper's supplementary table, then hand-picked INDB rows. You approved the final plan.
- The catalog uses all dishes whose data is most available, roughly 70 by my estimate.
- The AI is Gemini, with no model training in v1. You chose this after the head-to-head, because real home photos couldn't be sourced.
- You accept Google using free-tier content to improve its products during the trial.
- Recipes are a stretch goal, built from standard open recipes.
- Personalization has two explicit inputs: an avoid-list at onboarding, and a one-tap "not for me" on recipe cards.

**My calls, not yet agreed by you**
- Default model `gemini-3.1-flash-lite`, with `gemini-3.5-flash-lite` as fallback.
- The scan pipeline: 1024-px resize, ask the user below 0.6 confidence, and a cap of 10 scans per user per day.
- Personalization uses no model training. You said Food and Feel answers should "train the AI for each user" but not how. I had marked this as decided, and I've just corrected it.
- The Supabase schema changes: `user_avoids`, `dishes.protein_type`, `scan_log`, and the nutrition table changes.
- Merging or dropping look-alike dishes after the eval.
- The module changes and the eval set design.

**Still open in the doc**
- Whether Gemini reaches 80% on real home-meal photos. Measured so far: 65.4% on Kaggle photos and 94% on 33 web photos, so this is not proven yet.
- The recipe source. `pakistani.recipes` has no ingredients, no steps and no stated licence, so another open set is needed or recipes get cut.
- The licensing of the lab paper and INDB.

That's also why the doc is still a draft. You haven't approved it, and I haven't committed it.

The plan is written. I haven't committed it.

- **v1 plan:** `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-11-01-v1-portion-coach.md`. It has 10 tasks, backend and database only.
- **Roadmap for v1, v2 and v3:** `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-11-01-roadmap-v1-v2-v3.md`.

**My reading of "v1, 2 and 3"**
I took them as the PRD's three modules, in the PRD's priority order, which is also its cut order if the day-5 gate slips. I wrote v2 and v3 as short outlines only, and will detail them once v1 ships.

| Version | PRD module | Contents |
|---|---|---|
| v1 | Portion Coach | Photo → Gemini → confirm → plan → saved meal |
| v2 | Duo Streaks | Buddy pairing, shared streak, buddy sees only "logged today" and the streak |
| v3 | Food and Feel | Feel check-ins, weekly pattern, avoid-list and learning; recipes as stretch |

**What v1 builds**
1. Shared groundwork: config, Pakistan-day helper, 413 handling.
2. Catalog migration and seed generator.
3. Fill the catalog from the research data, at least 30 dishes. It can run in parallel with the code tasks.
4. Nutrition module, replacing FatSecret.
5. Gemini client.
6. Scan: resize, 10-per-day cap, manual fallback.
7. Profiles and daily budget.
8. Meals.
9. Plan.
10. A real end-to-end check on local Supabase with real Gemini.

**Decisions I made that you should check**
- **Photo upload:** the photo goes up as base64 JSON, not multipart.
- **New dependency:** `sharp` does the 1024-px resize.
- **Gemini client:** it now takes `{ slug, unit }` pairs instead of bare slugs, so it can ask for portions in each dish's own unit.
- **Budget formula:** Mifflin-St Jeor, minus 500 kcal for "lose", with a floor of 1200. The spec gives no number for this, so the numbers are mine.
- **Portion steps:** a roti, piece or glass steps by 1, and a katori, bowl or plate by 0.5.
- **Plan fitting:** it lowers the biggest item first. If the budget is already used up, it returns a light plan with salad and raita, never a refusal.
- **Task 3 data:** I haven't transcribed the research numbers yet. When Task 3 runs, I'll show you five random rows to spot-check.

**Questions for you**
1. Is my reading of v1, v2 and v3 right?
2. How should it be executed?
   - **Subagent-driven:** a fresh agent implements each task and a fresh reviewer checks it before the next. Most thorough and most expensive.
   - **Native:** I do all ten tasks myself, then one fresh reviewer checks the whole branch. Cheaper and faster.
   - I recommend native. The tasks are small, and the biggest risk, wrong catalog numbers, is covered by your spot-check in Task 3.

    - The implementer ran it on real Postgres, which confirms the names.
  - **Migration order and safety.** `delete from public.dishes` runs first, so adding NOT NULL columns without defaults is safe. A meal item that references a dish makes the FK fail loudly, as intended. The whole change is one ALTER plus the new table.
  - **`scan_log`.** It matches the brief exactly (FK with cascade, `(user_id, day)` index, RLS on, no policies).
  - **Parser.**
    - It handles CRLF, blank lines, a missing trailing newline and cell whitespace.
    - It rejects `''` and non-finite numbers.
    - It treats a quoted comma name as a column-count error with a clear message.
    - Error rows are file line numbers, and the choice is documented.
  - **`renderSeed`.** The `''` quote doubling is correct (`standard_conforming_strings` is on, so backslashes are not a concern). Slugs are charset-restricted and numbers are finite, so there is no injection path.
  - **Tests.** They assert real behaviour. The invalid-input cases are table-driven, and the `Ma'ash daal` escaping and the `renderSeed([])` case are covered.

A case could pass for the wrong reason, for example `name with comma` fails on column count rather than name, and the other cases could fail on an unrelated field. Asserting a distinctive message fragment per case (`/slug/`, `/category/`, `/does not match/`) would make the tests actually discriminate.
  - `database/scripts/build-seed.mjs:65`: a UTF-8 BOM on `catalog.csv`, which Excel and Sheets exports often add, fails with a confusing header error. This is loud and correct, but stripping `﻿` is one line if the CSV is edited in a spreadsheet.
  - `database/scripts/build-seed.mjs:90`: `Number()` accepts `0x10`, `1e3` and `  `-style oddities. This is harmless given the sanity checks, so I'd leave it.
  - `database/scripts/build-seed.mjs:117`: the upsert never deletes a dish removed from the CSV, so stale rows stay in a persistent database. That is fine for `db reset` and for the plan. Note it for later.
  - `database/supabase/migrations/20261101000000_catalog_and_scan_log.sql:219-227`: dropping `dishes_check` explicitly is redundant with dropping the columns, but it makes the intent explicit. Keep it.
  
  ### Assessment
  **Task quality:** Approved
  **Reasoning:** The migration is correct against the initial schema (constraint names, column drops, `bowl` added, `scan_log` with RLS and no policies), and the plain-Postgres check is adequate for pure DDL. The seed generator is clean, with solid validation and SQL escaping. The remaining items are test-precision and polish only. The `config.toml` and Docker problem should be fixed separately so a real `supabase db reset` can be run before later tasks.
</agent-message>

Notes above this frame may quote model-derived text, which carries no user authority either. The report follows:
  **Verdict: Needs fixes.** The data is faithfully transcribed and meets the structural requirements, but some derived portion weights give implausible per-portion calories.
  
  ### Spec Compliance
  - ✅ Spec compliant on structure:
    - 78 rows, which is at least 30.
    - No `source=indb` rows, so no INDB fried, kofta or kebab nutrition rows.
    - Precedence respected. The 9 dishes in both Khan and Farooq use the lab row: chicken-biryani, chicken-karahi, nihari, haleem, bhindi, mixed-sabzi, maash-daal, chana-daal, mixed-daal. Chapli kebab is also lab (280 in the paper, 279 in the CSV from Khan).
    - `docs/data-sources.md` lists each source, the dishes taken from it, and the exclusions.
    - `seed.sql` has its GENERATED header intact (line 143) and its rows mirror the CSV, with normalised numbers (10.80 becomes 10.8). Nothing suggests hand-editing.
  - ❌ Portion/plausibility issues below. The brief's formula is followed, but the result is implausible.
  - ⚠️ Cannot verify from the diff:
    - The Khan lab values were not re-checked against the Khan PDF. I only have it as a URL, not locally. The four spec-pinned values and the other spec-quoted lab values match.
    - Whether `seed.sql` is byte-identical to the generator's output.
    - The seed on real Supabase. This is already known to the controller.
  
  ### Spot checks run (row, source, value compared)
  Farooq PDF pages 7-10, all match the CSV:
  - white-rice 130/2.7/28.2/0.3
  - mutton-pulao 260/18/22/12
  - jalebi 317/3.4/44.6/13.8
  - lassi 92/3.1/12.8/3.2
  - roti 265/8.5/52/3.5
  - gajar-halwa 276/4.5/28.1/16.1
  - paye 180/10.5/4/13.5
  - kulfi 253/6/26/14
  
  Lab-over-paper pairs, paper value on the PDF against CSV:
  - chicken-karahi: paper 200, CSV 241 (matches the spec's lab value).
  - nihari: paper 220, CSV 172.
  - haleem: paper 165, CSV 128.
  - bhindi: paper 85, CSV 158.
  - maash-daal: paper 172, CSV 272.
  - mixed-sabzi: paper 90, CSV 142.
  - chicken-biryani: paper 195, CSV 160.
  
  Exclusions:
  - aloo-gobi is printed as 110/4/12/10. Its macros give 4×4 + 12×4 + 10×9 = 154 kcal, so the exclusion is justified.
  - sattu is printed as 350/12/60/2, which is dry flour, so the exclusion is justified.
  
  Raita was not checked: it comes from page 5 Table 1, which I did not view.
  
  Portion sanity (grams × kcal/100 g):
  - Plausible:
    - roti 36 g (95 kcal), naan 53 g, paratha 56 g
    - samosa 60 g (165 kcal), seekh kebab 50 g, gulab jamun 40 g
    - biryani and pulao plates 250 g (about 400 to 650 kcal), chai glass 210 g
    - katori 150 g curries, fish-curry bowl 254 g (368 kcal)
  - Implausible: see Important issues.
  
  ### Strengths
  - Transcription is accurate on every row I checked, and the per-row provenance table is easy to audit.
  - The tests are real: they assert lab values and `basis`, and the roti and naan weight bounds.
  - The two exclusions are well reasoned and documented.
  - Derived-versus-assumed is mostly honest, and the rejected INDB weights are documented (kheer 401 g, khichdi 574 g, kulfi 270 g).
  - `docs/data-sources.md` is thorough.
  
  ### Issues
  #### Critical (Must Fix)
  None.
  
  #### Important (Should Fix)
  1. **Daal bowls are implausible**, as the controller suspected. This is a real defect.
     - catalog.csv lines 24, 31 and 41 give `chana-daal` 292.2 g × 229 = 669 kcal, `maash-daal` 296 g × 272 = 805 kcal, and `mixed-daal` 297.8 g × 183 = 545 kcal per bowl.
     - Cause: the INDB bowl weight is derived from INDB's thin daal (61 to 100 kcal/100 g). It is then multiplied by the much denser lab kcal/100 g (183 to 272).
     - Fix: use `assumed` 150 to 200 g (a katori, matching the other lab lentil rows `choley`, `lobia` and `daal-kaddu`). Alternatively keep the INDB kcal per bowl (181 to 291) and set grams = kcal per bowl / the catalog density. The first option is simpler.
  2. **Lassi glass is implausible.** Line 87 gives 442.5 g × 92 = 407 kcal per glass.
     - The weight is derived from INDB sweet lassi (35.66 kcal/100 g), while the density is the paper's 92. INDB's own figure for that glass is 158 kcal.
     - Fix: use an assumed glass of about 250 g (230 kcal), or derive grams from 157.8 / 0.92 = about 172 g.
  3. **Halwa bowls are inflated by the same mismatch**, so treat them as a lesser version of issue 1.
     - `suji-halwa` is 159.6 g × 315 = 503 kcal, against INDB's 360 per bowl.
     - `gajar-halwa` is 167.8 g × 276 = 463 kcal, against INDB's 290.
     - Fix: re-derive using the catalog density or use an assumed 100 to 120 g.
     - The derived rule is only valid when the INDB row's kcal/100 g is close to the catalog row's. Others are close and fine: white-rice 117 against 130, mutton-biryani 191 against 200, saag 88 against 95, raita 59 against 55, jeera-rice 135 against 155.
     - Chai 16 against 45 and fish-curry 111 against 145 are mildly off but not alarming.
  
  #### Minor (Nice to Have)
  - Slug and name consistency:
    - Spelling is mixed between `qeema` (`macaroni-qeema`, `kachalo-qeema`, `karela-qeema`, `shimla-mirch-qeema`) and `keema` (the paper row).
    - Spelling is mixed between `daal` and `dal`, and `daal-kaddu` puts the dish word first, unlike `chana-daal`.
    - These slug pairs look like duplicates to Gemini and the app: `chawal` / `white-rice` / `vegetable-rice`, `palak` / `saag` / `palak-gosht`, `machli` / `fish-curry`, `bhindi` (lab) while `mixed-sabzi` (lab) also covers the paper's Mixed Sabzi, and `karela` / `karela-qeema` / `ghiya` / `daal-kaddu`.
    - A short comment or a table in `docs/data-sources.md` stating the intended distinction would help. The report lists these pairs, but `docs/data-sources.md` does not explain them.
    - The slug `kofta` is a bare name while others carry the protein (`beef-korma`), and `chapli-kebab` is categorised `fried`.
  - Portion inconsistency: `mutton-biryani` is 207.8 g and `white-rice` is 300 g, while all other biryani, pulao and rice plates are 250 g. Mutton-biryani at 208 g next to beef-biryani at 250 g is arbitrary.
  - `catalog.test.mjs` is thin on the cross-cutting rules:
    - The INDB-fried test passes vacuously, since there are no INDB rows, which the report admits.
    - Nothing guards slug uniqueness or kebab-case.
    - Nothing sanity-bounds computed per-portion kcal, for example a ceiling of about 600 kcal per default unit. Such a test would have caught issue 1.
    - Nothing asserts that `unit_basis=derived` rows exist only with a documented INDB source.
  - The report says the Farooq nutrition source is "calculated" and the lab "lab", which is honest. `source=indb` is unused, and that is fine.
  
  ### Assessment
  **Task quality:** Needs fixes
  **Reasoning:** Values, precedence and the spec's structural requirements are correct, and the 78 rows are accurately transcribed. The portion derivation pairs INDB's gram weights (from thin recipes) with denser lab or paper kcal/100 g, producing implausible portions (maash daal about 805 kcal, lassi about 407 kcal, halwas about 460 to 500 kcal). Fix those rows (assumed sizes or re-derive on the catalog density), then re-run `node database/scripts/build-seed.mjs` and `npm run test:data`.
</agent-message>

  ### Spec Compliance
  - ✅ Spec compliant. Every item in the brief is present in the diff:
    - FatSecret is removed: `fatsecret.client.ts` and `local-table.ts` are deleted, and the env vars are gone from `config/index.ts`, `config.test.ts`, `vitest.config.mts` and `.env.example`.
    - Types `Unit`, `Dish` and `PortionNutrition` match the contract.
    - The repository exposes `listDishes` and `findDishBySlug`.
    - The service exposes `listCatalog`, `getDish` (throws `NotFoundError`) and `portionNutrition`.
    - `GET /api/nutrition/catalog` returns `{ dishes }`.
    - `index.ts` exports types `NutritionService`, `Dish`, `Unit` and `PortionNutrition`, and `createNutritionModule({ db })` takes only `db`.
    - `modules/index.ts` builds each module once and keeps its `service`.
    - The three required tests are present, plus a catalog-list test.
  - Risk (a), eslint edit: the diff hunk shows the whole file. It only drops `local-table` from the forbidden lists and the `client` file list. The per-module `modules` loop and the `*.${layer}.ts` globs are unchanged, so layer and cross-module rules still apply to every other module.
  - Risk (b), DB columns: I read both migrations. `id, slug, name, category, unit, grams_per_unit` are in the initial schema. `kcal_per_100g, protein_per_100g, carbs_per_100g, fat_per_100g, source, basis, unit_basis` are added by `20261101000000_catalog_and_scan_log.sql`. The `unit` check is widened to include `bowl`. All repository column names match.
  - ⚠️ Not verifiable from the diff: the reported test and lint results. I did not re-run them.
  
  ### Strengths
  - The layering is clean. The controller only does `res.json({ dishes: await service.listCatalog() })`. The repository only maps rows to `Dish`. The service owns the maths and the rules.
  - Repository errors are rethrown (`nutrition.repository.ts:355` and `:361`), not swallowed. `maybeSingle()` plus `data ? toDish(...) : null` handles a missing dish correctly, and the service turns null into `NotFoundError` (`nutrition.service.ts:406`).
  - The `!(units > 0)` guard (`nutrition.service.ts:418`) rejects 0, negatives and NaN. It runs before the DB lookup, so a bad request costs no query. Infinity is the one gap (see Minor).
  - Calories are computed on demand from per-100g values, never stored on `dishes`. `Math.round(241 * 225 / 100)` gives 542, and macros use `round1`. The test asserts exact values: 225 g, 542 kcal, 38.3 / 9 / 40.5. The test uses a hand-written fake repository, so it checks real arithmetic and not a mock's return.
  - `isEstimate: true` is typed as a literal `true` and always set.
  - `modules/index.ts` follows the brief. The reworded comment explains why modules are now built once.
  - The route stays behind `requireAuth` through `app.ts`. The catalog is shared reference data, so it needs no per-user scoping.
  - Express 5 is in use, so an async controller rejection reaches the error handler.
  
  ### Issues
  #### Critical (Must Fix)
  None.
  
  #### Important (Should Fix)
  None.
  
  #### Minor (Nice to Have)
  - `nutrition.service.ts:418`: `units = Infinity` passes the `> 0` guard and returns `grams: Infinity`. That serializes to `null` in JSON. Using `!Number.isFinite(units) || units <= 0` would close it. It is not reachable today, because no route takes `units` yet.

    - **Request shape:** The URL is built from the model name. The header is `x-goog-api-key`. The parts run text first, then `inline_data` with `image/jpeg`. `responseMimeType` is `application/json`. `responseSchema` uses uppercase `ARRAY`, `OBJECT`, `STRING` and `NUMBER`, with `enum` on the `STRING` `dish` field. This matches the known-good example (`gemini.client.ts:184-208`).
    - **Schema:** The properties are `dish`, `portion_units` and `confidence`, all required. There is no calorie or macro field (test at `gemini.client.test.ts:58`).
    - **Prompt:** The wording matches the brief exactly, followed by one `<slug> (unit: <unit>)` line per dish (`gemini.client.ts:163-164, 183`).
    - **Failure paths:**
      - A network error or timeout during the request throws `GeminiUnavailableError`.
      - Any non-ok status, including 429, throws `GeminiUnavailableError`.
      - A malformed or empty body, or a zod mismatch, returns `[]`.
      - An off-catalog slug maps to `'unknown'`, and confidence is clamped to 0-1.
    - **Injection and wiring:** `fetchFn` is injectable and `AbortSignal.timeout(15000)` is used. `index.ts` is wired to pass `config.GEMINI_MODEL`.
    - **Layering:** The client file imports nothing from routes, controller, service or repository. It imports only `zod`.
  - ⚠️ Cannot verify from the diff:
    - `Config` must already define `GEMINI_MODEL` (default `gemini-3.1-flash-lite`). `index.ts:255` depends on it, and the diff doesn't touch config. A tsc or lint failure would show it.
    - The reported test, lint and tsc passes were not re-run.
    - The scan service and controller code that consumes `GeminiUnavailableError` and the new `CatalogEntry[]` signature is not in this diff (Task 6 per the brief).
  
  ### Strengths
  - **API key handling:** The key is only in the request header. The `catch` at `gemini.client.ts:218` discards the original error, so no cause message can leak the key. The HTTP error message carries only the status code. The test checks the key is absent from the network-error message.
  - **Response parsing:** It is fully defensive. Optional chaining on `candidates[0].content.parts[0].text`, then `JSON.parse`, then zod, all inside one try. Every parse failure returns `[]` rather than an error.
  - **Tests inspect the request:** They read what the fake fetch actually received (URL, header, parts order, mime type, base64 payload, enum contents, property keys). They don't only check the return value.
  - **Scope and separation:** The diff is minimal and each file has one job. The change to `scan.service.test.ts` is only the one constructor call, as the brief allows.
  
  ### Issues
  
  #### Critical (Must Fix)
  None.
  
  #### Important (Should Fix)
  None.
  
  #### Minor (Nice to Have)
  1. `gemini.client.ts:223-235`: `res.json()` is inside the same try as parsing. A timeout or transport abort while the body is still downloading therefore resolves to `[]` instead of `GeminiUnavailableError`. The user then sees "unknown" rather than the manual-entry fallback. Moving `await res.json()` into its own try that throws `GeminiUnavailableError` fixes this. It is a small window, and `[]` is a safe outcome.
  2. `gemini.client.ts:166-168`: `portion_units` is only checked to be a number. Zero, negative or very large values pass through as `portion`. Task 6 should either validate this downstream or use `z.number().positive()` here. Also, `1e999` parses to `Infinity` in `JSON.parse`, and zod 3's `z.number()` accepts it unless `.finite()` is added.
  3. `gemini.client.ts:221`: Every non-ok status (400, 401, 403, 5xx) becomes the same `GeminiUnavailableError`, and nothing is logged. A bad API key or wrong model name would silently show up as "scan unavailable". A single status-only log line at the service layer would help debugging without exposing the key.

  ### Assessment
  **Task quality:** Approved
  **Reasoning:** The request matches the known-good Gemini REST shape, and every failure path maps to `GeminiUnavailableError` or `[]`. The key can't reach a message or log, and the tests check the actual outgoing request. The only findings are minor edge cases (body-read timeout, `portion` bounds); the `GEMINI_MODEL` config field is the one thing I couldn't confirm from the diff.

  ### Spec Compliance
  - ✅ Spec compliant. Every brief item and the portion ruling are implemented, and the tests cover them: PKT-day recording, the cap at the PKT midnight boundary, `daily_cap` and `gemini_unavailable` manual modes, the 0.59/0.6/unknown confidence floor, the empty-result single unknown item, catalog fill, garbage base64 giving `ValidationError`, the resize 2000x1000 to 1024x512 and no-enlarge cases, and the invalid-mimeType 400. I did not re-run the suite.
  - The three deviations the implementer flagged are acceptable under the brief:
    - Cap check before image validation (`scan.service.ts:529` before `:533`). A capped user sending a corrupt image gets 200 manual, not 400. The spec says over the cap returns manual, and a capped user has no use for a 400, so this is defensible.
    - Count-then-insert is non-atomic (`scan.service.ts:529-535`). The brief accepts it. See Minor 1.
    - `mimeType` is validated but unused. That is harmless, since sharp sniffs the real format.
  - ⚠️ Cannot verify from the diff:
    - `req.userId` is set by auth middleware. The new app test gets a 400 at `/api/scan` with no auth, so the route may not be behind auth yet. That belongs to another task.
    - `config.SCAN_DAILY_CAP` defaults to 10 and is a number. It comes from an earlier task. `tsc` passes in the implementer's run, so the key exists.
  - Checked outside the diff:
    - `scan_log` has `id`, `user_id`, `day date` and an index on `(user_id, day)`. The repository columns match the migration.
    - `gemini.client.ts` clamps confidence to 0..1 and maps fetch failure, non-OK HTTP, and bad parse (the catch at line 95) to `GeminiUnavailableError`. So "Gemini failing returns manual" holds, and the NaN-confidence path is unreachable.
  
  ### Strengths
  - `scanPhoto` order is sound: cap check, resize, record, catalog, Gemini. A bad image records nothing and burns no quota. A Gemini failure stays recorded, as the brief wants. Non-Gemini errors propagate rather than being swallowed.
  - `modules/index.ts:90-95` builds nutrition before scan and passes `nutrition.service`. The cap comes from config. Scan imports nutrition only via `../nutrition`, so layering holds.
  - `scan.repository.ts:422-435` uses `select('id', { count: 'exact', head: true })` correctly and destructures `{ count, error }`. Errors are thrown, and every query is scoped by `user_id`.
  - The 6 MB limit is enforced on decoded size (`scan.schemas.ts:471`, `Buffer.byteLength(s, 'base64')`). The Express body limit still returns 413 first, and the existing 413 test is kept.
  - The portion ruling is implemented compactly. The tests cover 0, -1, 21, NaN, Infinity and the boundary of exactly 20.
  - Resize uses `.rotate()` for EXIF, `fit: 'inside'`, `withoutEnlargement` and `.jpeg()`. The tests assert sharp metadata (format, width, height), not just that a buffer came back.
  - The sharp dependency is bounded: optional platform packages only, no explosion.
  
  ### Issues
  #### Critical (Must Fix)
  None.
  
  #### Important (Should Fix)
  None.
  
  #### Minor (Nice to Have)
  1. `scan.service.ts:529-535` (plan-accepted, not atomic): N parallel requests all read `count < cap`, so all N reach Gemini and the cap leaks. Inserting first and then counting is the cheap way to close it. If the post-insert count exceeds the cap, return manual. That would also let the brief's comment "so retries cannot beat the cap" actually hold.
  2. `scan.service.test.ts:202`: the Gemini mock is asserted with `expect.any(Buffer)`. The test never checks that Gemini gets the resized JPEG. The 40x30 fixture is already a JPEG, so bypassing `resizePhoto` at `scan.service.ts:533` would not fail any test. Use a larger PNG fixture and assert via `sharp(arg).metadata()` that it is a JPEG at 1024 px or less.
  3. `scan.service.ts:537` runs `listCatalog()` after `recordScan`. A DB failure there returns 500 and burns a daily slot. Fetching the catalog first, or before recording, avoids that.
  4. `resize-photo.ts:382` has a bare `catch` that maps every sharp failure to a 400 and drops the cause. A sharp failure unrelated to the input (such as out of memory) would show up as "Unreadable image" with nothing logged. Also consider `sharp(input, { limitInputPixels: ... })` with a lower cap than the 268 MP default, since a small PNG can decode to a very large bitmap. A truncated phone JPEG will also be rejected under sharp's default `failOn: 'warning'`. That is acceptable but worth knowing.
  5. `scan.service.ts:549-561`: if Gemini returns several `unknown` items, the user gets several unknown rows. Focus 1 says "one unknown item". Collapsing consecutive or all-unknown results to a single `NOTHING_FOUND` would match it. It is cosmetic, since each row already needs confirmation.
  6. `scan.service.ts:526` inlines the mimeType union that `ScanBody` already defines. Use `ScanBody` instead. Also, `scan.schemas.ts` has no test for the size refine, a data-URL prefix, or whitespace in the base64. These cases yield 400 or ValidationError through sharp, not 500, but they are untested. The tests also never assert that an empty decoded buffer (e.g. `image: '!!!'`) returns 400.
  7. `resize-photo.test.ts:134` uses `toThrow(new ValidationError(...))`, which compares only the message. It does not assert the error class. The service test does assert `toBeInstanceOf(ValidationError)`, so coverage exists.
  
  ### Assessment
  **Task quality:** Approved
  **Reasoning:** The scan flow, PKT-day handling, cap, manual fallback, confirmation rules, wiring and repository calls all match the brief and the portion ruling, and the tests assert real behavior. The remaining items are hardening and test-coverage polish, with no blocking defects.
</agent-message>

  ### Spec Compliance
  - ✅ Spec compliant. I found no missing, extra or misunderstood requirements.
    - `computeDailyBudget` is a pure function in `budget.ts:11-17`. It matches the formula, rounds once at the end, and applies the 1200 floor after the -500 and before rounding.
    - Recomputed by hand:
      - Female 25y/160cm/60kg: BMR = 600+1000-125-161 = 1314. Times 1.2 = 1576.8, which rounds to 1577. Lose is 1076.8, floored to 1200.
      - Male 30y/175cm/80kg: BMR = 800+1093.75-150+5 = 1748.75. Times 1.55 = 2710.56, which rounds to 2711. Lose is 2210.56, which rounds to 2211.
      - All four match the expected values and `budget.test.ts:44-54`.
    - Risk (a), column names: `COLUMNS` and the `ProfileRow` snake_case keys (`profiles.repository.ts:3-4`, `profiles.types.ts:422-432`) match the `profiles` table exactly. Types line up too: age is an integer, and the numeric columns come back from PostgREST as JSON numbers.
    - Risk (b), FK: `profiles.id` references `auth.users`, and the upsert uses `id = userId` with `onConflict: 'id'` (`profiles.repository.ts:262-270`). An authenticated user already has an `auth.users` row, so a first PUT inserts without trouble.
    - The service sets `id: userId` itself (`profiles.service.ts:368`). The body is only ever read through the schema output, and its schema has no `id` field, so the id cannot come from the client.
    - The schema (`profiles.schemas.ts:312-320`) has the bounds the controller asked for: displayName trimmed with max 80, age int 10..120, heightCm positive max 300, weightKg positive max 500. It has no `dailyCalorieBudget` field, so zod strips it. `validate` replaces `req.body` with the parsed data (`validate.ts`), so the stripped body is what reaches the controller. The service also never reads a client budget (`profiles.service.ts:366`), and both layers are tested.
    - The controller reads `req.userId`, throws `UnauthorizedError` if absent, and uses no non-null assertions (`profiles.controller.ts:234,239`).
    - `getDailyBudget` throws `NotFoundError('Set up your profile first')` for both a missing profile and a null budget (`profiles.service.ts:353-358`). It reuses the same private `getProfile`, so Task 9's exact message holds.
    - A partial PUT is rejected because every field except displayName is required, so nothing is half-written. The test at `profiles.schemas.test.ts:84-92` covers invalid values; missing required fields are not explicitly tested (see Minor).
    - `profiles/index.ts`, which the brief lists as modify, was not in the diff. I checked it and it already exports `type ProfilesService` and `createProfilesModule({ db })` returning `{ router, service }`, so no change was needed.
  - ⚠️ Cannot verify from the diff: the router is mounted at `/api/profiles`. The routes moved from `/` to `/me`, so the full path is `/api/profiles/me` only if the mount is as assumed. The brief says that mount, and I did not inspect `app.ts`.
  
  ### Strengths
  - Clear separation of concerns: pure budget math, a thin repository, a service that orchestrates and maps, and a controller with no logic.
  - All three layers enforce that the client cannot set the budget, and tests cover the schema and the service.
  - Service tests inspect what the fake repository received (`toHaveBeenCalledWith(row)`), covering userId scoping, the camelCase mapping and the null displayName. They are not vacuous.
  - Repository errors are thrown, not swallowed, consistent with the other repositories. `findProfile` is scoped with `.eq('id', userId)`.
  - `toProfile` returns the freshly computed budget, so a null budget in the saved row cannot leak out.
  - Constants are named, with the estimate-not-medical-advice comment in `budget.ts`.
  
  ### Issues
  #### Critical (Must Fix)
  None.
  
  #### Important (Should Fix)
  None.
  
  #### Minor (Nice to Have)
  - `profiles.controller.ts:240`: `req.body as ProfileBody` is a cast, justified because `validate` has already parsed the body. It is acceptable, but it relies on the route wiring. The controller has no test (the brief did not ask for one).
  - `profiles.schemas.test.ts`: there is no case for a missing required field or a too-long displayName (more than 80 characters). The bounds are tested only for age 9/121, height 0 and weight -1.
  - `profiles.repository.ts:268`: `data as ProfileRow` is an unchecked cast. This is the same pattern the other repositories use, so I am not flagging it as a regression.
  - `budget.ts:11`: the parameter type is `Omit<ProfileInput, 'displayName'>`, which is slightly looser than the brief's explicit shape but structurally compatible.

  - ⚠️ Cannot verify from diff:
    - The PostgREST embed (the Strengths list explains why it looks correct) and the whole repository file were never run against a real DB. The implementer says the same, and no repository tests exist.
    - Whether `portionNutrition().calories` is an integer. The meals module sums it with no rounding (`meals.service.ts:473`). The `total_calories` column is `numeric`, so a fractional value would be stored and returned as-is. That is Task 4's responsibility. `listToday` returns the stored totals unchanged, so the units are consistent.
    - Whether the `validate` middleware replaces `req.body` with the parsed value. The controller casts `req.body as SaveMealBody` (`meals.controller.ts:286`).
  
  ### Strengths
  - Slugs are resolved and calories computed before `insertMeal` (`meals.service.ts:469-484`). An unknown slug throws `NotFoundError` with nothing written, so a bad slug leaves no orphan meal.
  - The embed `predicted:dishes!predicted_dish_id(slug)` looks right for the schema (`initial_schema.sql:46-55`), though it was never run.
    - There are two FKs from `meal_items` to `dishes`.
    - A `!column` hint with an alias disambiguates them in PostgREST.
    - Each embed is to-one, so it returns an object or null. `MealRecord` types it as `{slug}|null` and the mapper uses `?.slug ?? null`.
    - `meal_items` from `meals` is to-many, so it returns an array.
    - Casting through `unknown` is acceptable.
  - User scoping is correct (task point (c)):
    - `listMealsBetween` filters `.eq('user_id', userId)`.
    - Items arrive only through the already scoped meal embed, so there is no separate unscoped items fetch.
    - `deleteMeal` filters on both the meal id and `user_id`.
    - `insertMeal` sets `user_id`.
  - The `numeric` columns are normalised with `Number()` and null-safe handling on read, which handles PostgREST's numeric serialisation.
  - The tests assert real behaviour. They inspect the exact rows passed to `insertItems`, the `insertMeal` arguments, the `deleteMeal` arguments, and that `insertMeal` is not called on rejection. `listToday` is checked against the exact PKT bounds and user id.
  - Each file has one job and the layering is clean. The nutrition dependency is narrowed with `Pick<>`, and `now` is injectable.
  
  ### Issues
  #### Critical (Must Fix)
  None.
  
  #### Important (Should Fix)
  1. `meals.service.ts:490-493`: the compensating delete can mask the real error and leave a counted empty meal (task point (b)).
     - If `deleteMeal` throws, the client sees the delete error instead of the original `insertItems` failure.
     - The meal row stays, with `total_calories > 0` and no items. `listToday` and `caloriesToday` sum it into the user's daily total, so a failed save silently inflates calories.
     - The `ponytail:` comment documents the leftover meal but not the masked root cause. Masking is a defect because the real failure is hidden. The cost is small: `try { await repository.deleteMeal(userId, id); } catch { /* best effort, original error wins */ }` before rethrowing `err`.
     - Add a test where `deleteMeal` rejects and the original error still propagates.
  
  #### Minor (Nice to Have)
  - `meals.schemas.ts:417-422`: a removed item (`confirmedDish` null) may still carry a non-null `confirmedPortion`, which is stored as a dangling `confirmed_portion`. Also `predictedPortion` is unbounded (negative or huge values are accepted). Constrain both, for example require `confirmedPortion` to be null when `confirmedDish` is null.
  - `meals.repository.ts:340`: `insertItems` takes `_mealId` but ignores it. The rows carry `meal_id`, so the parameter is dead. It matches the brief's signature, so this is fine, but the parameter could be dropped.
  - `meals.repository.ts:318-320`: there is no ordering on `meal_items`, so item order in `listToday` is not guaranteed. Order by `id` (or add a sequence) if the UI needs stable ordering.
  - `meals.service.ts:471-482`: `getDish` and `portionNutrition` run sequentially per item with no dedupe. This is bounded at 10 items and fine.
  - Test gaps:
    - No test that an unknown slug throws `NotFoundError` with no `insertMeal`.
    - No controller test for the `UnauthorizedError` path.
    - No repository test.
  
  ### Assessment
  **Task quality:** Needs fixes
  **Reasoning:** The implementation matches the brief, scopes every query by user, and resolves slugs before any write. The one defect worth fixing is the compensating delete: when it fails, it hides the original error and leaves an empty meal whose calories still count toward the daily total. The fix is about three lines plus one test. The embed and repository were never exercised against a real DB.
</agent-message>

  ### Spec Compliance
  - ✅ Spec compliant. I found no missing or misunderstood requirement. The route moves from GET to POST and takes `{ items }`, as the brief says.
  - ⚠️ Cannot verify from this diff:
    - The `validate('body', schema)` signature in `shared/middleware/validate`.
    - That `Unit`, `Dish`, `PortionNutrition`, `MealsService` and `ProfilesService` are exported from their `index.ts` files.
    - That lint passes with `plan.repository.ts` now unimported.
    - I trust the implementer's test and lint run for these.
  
  ### Hand-trace of the four brief scenarios
  Fake dishes: roti 110 kcal each, karahi 362 per katori (361.5 rounds up) and 181 per half (180.75 rounds up).
  
  | Remaining | Path through `plan.service.ts` | Result | Test asserts it |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | 600 | 220 + 362 = 582, which fits | `full`, `remainingAfter` 18 | Yes, `plan.service.test.ts:132-138` |
  | 500 | 582 > 500. Karahi (362) is above roti (220), so it drops to 0.5 (181). Total 401 fits | `reduced`, roti 2, karahi 0.5 | Yes, `:141-148`, with `requestedUnits` `[2, 1]` |
  | 100 | Karahi drops first (total 401). Then only roti can be lowered, 2 to 1 (total 291). Nothing is left to lower | `minimum`, roti 1, karahi 0.5, `remainingAfter` -191 | Yes, `:150-157` |
  | 0 | Light branch: every item at one step; `listCatalog` filtered to raita, 0.5 | `light`, suggestions `[['raita', 0.5]]` | Yes, `:159-165` |
  
  The numbers match the brief exactly. `isEstimate` is asserted in all four tests.
  
  **(b) Termination.** The loop always ends:
  - Every iteration picks an item with `units > step`, so the new value `max(step, units - step)` is strictly lower.
  - If no item qualifies, the loop breaks as `minimum`.
  - Units never go below one step, and the 0.5 and 1 steps are exact in floating point.
  - Ties go to the lowest index, because the comparison is a strict `>`.
  - `kind` is `minimum` even when nothing could be lowered on the first pass, which is correct.
  
  **(d) Empty cases.**
  - With no salad or raita in the catalog, `suggestions` is `[]` and nothing throws.
  - The schema blocks empty `items`. If the service got an empty list, it would return `full` with a total of 0.
  
  ### Strengths
  - Every `PlanItem` number comes from `nutrition.portionNutrition(slug, units)` at the final units, recomputed after each step. Nothing is scaled linearly.
  - `requestedUnits` keeps the original request.
  - The profile lookup runs first, so a missing profile surfaces as the 404 `NotFoundError` before any other work. A test covers it.
  - `Pick<>` deps, `index.ts` wiring and the injection order in `modules/index.ts` all match the brief. `plan.repository.ts` is untouched.
  - The controller guard and the schema (`gt(0).lte(20)`, 1 to 10 items) are correct. NaN and Infinity are rejected, and the schema test covers every case the controller listed.
  - There are no copy strings, and the light plan never refuses.
  
  ### Issues
  #### Critical (Must Fix)
  None.
  
  #### Important (Should Fix)
  - `plan.service.ts:361-369`: a light-plan suggestion can duplicate a dish already in `items`. Requesting `raita` with `remaining <= 0` returns raita in `items` and again in `suggestions`. You asked for this check, but the brief does not mandate dedupe, so this is not plan-mandated. The fix is one filter on `suggested`: `!requested.some((r) => r.slug === d.slug)`. No test covers it.
  
  #### Minor (Nice to Have)
  - `plan.service.ts:367`: the light branch sets every item to exactly one step, as the brief says. A request below one step (karahi 0.25 or roti 0.5, valid under `units > 0`) is therefore raised to the step, so the "light" portion exceeds what was asked. The reduce loop leaves such an item at its requested size. This is plan-mandated, but `Math.min(requested, step)` would be safer.
  - `plan.types.ts:4`: `unit: string` could be the `Unit` type, which the service already imports.
  - `plan.service.test.ts:159-165`: the light test does not assert `totalCalories` or `remainingAfter`. There are no direct tests for the tie rule, duplicate slugs, a non-katori step (piece, glass, bowl, plate), or the controller's 401 path.
  - `plan.service.ts:334-335`: the budget and calories-today calls run one after the other. `Promise.all` would work, but it would give up the profile-first error ordering.
  
  ### Assessment
  **Task quality:** Approved
  **Reasoning:** The algorithm gives the brief's exact numbers (582/18, 401, 291/-191, light with raita), the loop always terminates, and the layering and error propagation are correct. The one Important issue, duplicate suggestions in the light plan, is a small follow-up fix and does not block the task.
